Alphabet Transformation

1

Procedure

{\color[rgb]{0,0.2773,0.6719}{\tt AlphaTrans}}(M,A):=

2

match

A
:

3

case

\emptyset
or
\epsilon
or
\bullet

do

return

A
;

4

case

{\langle}{\mathit{\mathbf{\mathbf{op}}\,{\overline{x_{i}}}}\,\resizebox{234471}{0}{=}\,\nu\,|\,{\phi}}{\rangle}
or
{\langle}{\phi}{\rangle}

do

5

M^{\prime}\leftarrow\{{\langle}{\mathit{\mathbf{\mathbf{op}}\,{\overline{x_{i}}}}\,\resizebox{218453}{0}{=}\,\nu\,|\,{\phi^{\prime}}}{\rangle}\in M~|~\phi^{\prime}\implies\phi\}
;

6

return

\bigvee_{m\in M^{\prime}}{\tt Hash}(m)

7

case

A_{1};A_{2}

do

return

{\color[rgb]{0,0.2773,0.6719}{\tt AlphaTrans}}(M,A_{1});{\color[rgb]{0,0.2773,0.6719}{\tt AlphaTrans}}(M,A_{2})
;

8

case

A_{1}\lor A_{2}

do

return

{\color[rgb]{0,0.2773,0.6719}{\tt AlphaTrans}}(M,A_{1})\lor{\color[rgb]{0,0.2773,0.6719}{\tt AlphaTrans}}(M,A_{2})
;

9

case

A_{1}\land A_{2}

do

return

{\color[rgb]{0,0.2773,0.6719}{\tt AlphaTrans}}(M,A_{1})\land{\color[rgb]{0,0.2773,0.6719}{\tt AlphaTrans}}(M,A_{2})
;

10

case

A_{1}\setminus A_{2}

do

return

{\color[rgb]{0,0.2773,0.6719}{\tt AlphaTrans}}(M,A_{1})\setminus{\color[rgb]{0,0.2773,0.6719}{\tt AlphaTrans}}(M,A_{2})
;

11

case

A^{*}

do

return

{\color[rgb]{0,0.2773,0.6719}{\tt AlphaTrans}}(M,A_{1})^{*}
;

Alphabet Transformation

The alphabet transformation
function
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2773,0.6719}{\tt AlphaTrans}}
translates a SFA into a FA that shares the
same regular operators. Notably, the alphabet transformation for
effect events gathers all minterms that can imply the qualifier of the
effect event (line
17
); moreover, the effect event

{\langle}{\mathit{\mathbf{\mathbf{op}}\,{\overline{x_{i}}}}\,\resizebox{218453}{0}{=}\,\nu\,|\,{\phi}}{\rangle}
requires the gathered minterms
have the same operator (
\mathbf{op}
).

Algorithm 3

Ghost Variable Inference

1

Procedure

{\color[rgb]{0,0.2773,0.6719}{\tt Abduce}}(\Gamma,A,A^{\prime},\overline{x_{i}{:}b_{i}}):=

2

if

\Gamma,\overline{x_{i}{:}{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mbox{\rm{\lx@text@lbrace}}}{\nu{:}{b_{i}}\,|\,{\top}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mbox{\rm{\lx@text@rbrace}}}}\not\vdash A\subseteq A^{\prime}

then

return

\bot

;

3

L\leftarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.2773,0.6719}{\tt GetLits}}(\Gamma,A\lor B)
;

4

M^{?}\leftarrow\bigcup_{i}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2773,0.6719}{\tt BoolComb}}{}(L\cup x_{i})
;

5

M^{+}\leftarrow\emptyset
;
M^{-}\leftarrow\emptyset
;
\phi\leftarrow\top
;

6

while

\exists m\in M^{?}.m{\implies}\phi

do

7

M^{?}\leftarrow M^{?}\setminus\{m\}
;

8

\phi^{\prime}\leftarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.2773,0.6719}{\tt Synthesize}}(M^{+}\cup M^{?},M^{-}\cup\{m\})
;

9

if

\Gamma,\overline{x_{i}{:}{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mbox{\rm{\lx@text@lbrace}}}{\nu{:}{b_{i}}\,|\,{\top}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mbox{\rm{\lx@text@rbrace}}}},z{:}{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mbox{\rm{\lx@text@lbrace}}}{\mathbf{{\nu}}{:}{{\tt unit}}\,|\,{\phi^{\prime}}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mbox{\rm{\lx@text@rbrace}}}\not\vdash A\subseteq A^{\prime}

then

10

M^{-}\leftarrow M^{-}\cup\{m\}
;

11

\phi\leftarrow\phi^{\prime}
;

12

else

M^{+}\leftarrow M^{+}\cup\{m\}

;

13

foreach

x_{i}{:}b_{i}

do

14

M\leftarrow\{m\in{\color[rgb]{0,0.2773,0.6719}{\tt BoolComb}}{}(L_{\Gamma}\cup x_{i})~|~m{\implies}\phi\}
;

15

t_{i}\leftarrow{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mbox{\rm{\lx@text@lbrace}}}{\nu{:}{b_{i}}\,|\,{\bigvee_{m\in M}m[x_{i}\mapsto\nu]}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mbox{\rm{\lx@text@rbrace}}}
;

16

return

\overline{x_{i}{:}t_{i}}
;

Ghost Variable Instantiatation

As we show in

Algorithm 3
, the abductive synthesizer
Abduce

adapts an existing algorithm
(
Zhou et al., 2021
)
that synthesizes a

maximal
assignment of unknown qualifier
\phi_{i}
for the
given ghost variables
x_{i}{:}b_{i}
. These inferred qualifiers are
strong enough to prove the given inclusion relation between two
input automata under the given type context (i.e.,

\Gamma\vdash A\subseteq A^{\prime}
). Note that the ghost variables only
make sense when the inclusion relation holds with all unknown
formulae
\phi_{i}
are maximally weak (i.e., true) (line
2
).
Similar to the minterm algorithm in
Algorithm 2
, the
abductive algorithm gathers all literals that appear in the pre- and
post-condition automata and type context (line
3
) that will be
used to generate candidate minterms (feature vectors, in the
literature of abductive synthesis
(
Zhou et al., 2021
)
), to build the
hypothesis space for synthesis. The function
Abduce
uses a
counterexample-guided refinement loop (line
6
-
12
) adapted from
the existing algorithm
(
Zhou et al., 2021
)
manipulating three sets of
minterms: unknown minterms
M^{?}
, positive minterms

M^{+}
, and negative minterms
M^{-}
. Thanks to the
multi-abduction approach from the existing algorithm
(
Zhou et al., 2021
)
,
inferring a formula
\phi
from the union of all minterms induces
all the assignment of unknown qualifiers
\overline{\phi_{i}}
at one
shot. The auxiliary synthesizer
Synthesize
(line
8
) returns a
formula that can be implied by given positive minterm and unknown
minterms but cannot be implied by any given negative
minterms. Following the existing algorithm
(
Zhou et al., 2021
)
, function

Synthesize
is implemented as a Decision Tree (DT) that
classifies these two sets of minterms. On the other hand, the
inclusion query on line
9
decides if unknown minterms should be
positive or negative; it also guarantees the soundness of the
algorithm, that is, a synthesized type is always consistent with the
given automaton
A
and
A^{\prime}
. Finally, the function
Abduce

returns refinement type
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mbox{\rm{\lx@text@lbrace}}}{\nu{:}{b_{i}}\,|\,{\phi_{i}}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mbox{\rm{\lx@text@rbrace}}}
for each ghost
variable
x_{i}
, where
\phi_{i}
is the union of all local minterms
that can imply
\phi
(line
14
-
15
).

Appendix E
Proofs

Type Soundness

The Coq formalization of our core language, typing rules, and the proof of
Theorem 4.8
and Corollary
4.9
are available on Zenodo

https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.10806686

Soundness of Algorithmic Typing

We present the proof for
Theorem 5.1
from
Sec. 5
. The proof requires the following lemmas about the
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2773,0.6719}{\tt SFAInclusion}}
subroutine and the instantiation function
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2773,0.6719}{\tt Abduce}}
.

Lemma E.1.

[Soundness of Inclusion Algorithm] Given type context
\Gamma
, automaton
A
and
A^{\prime}
,

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.2773,0.6719}{\tt SFAInclusion}}(\Gamma,A,A^{\prime}){\implies}\Gamma\vdash A\subseteq A^{\prime}

Lemma E.2.

[Soundness of Instantiation Function] Given a type context
\Gamma
, ghost variables
\overline{x_{i}{:}b_{i}}
, automaton
A
and
A^{\prime}
,

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.2773,0.6719}{\tt Abduce}}(\Gamma,A,A^{\prime},\overline{x_{i}{:}b_{i}})\implies\Gamma,\overline{x_{i}{:}{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mbox{\rm{\lx@text@lbrace}}}{\nu{:}{b_{i}}\,|\,{\phi_{i}}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mbox{\rm{\lx@text@rbrace}}}}\vdash A\subseteq A^{\prime}

The soundness of
SFAInclusion
establishes that it is safe to treat the subtyping relation in our typing algorithm as in declarative type system. On the other hand, the soundness of instantiation function induces the following lemma.

Lemma E.3.

[Instantiation Implies Precondition Automata Inclusion] Given a type context
\Gamma
, term
e
, consider the following the refinement type that has ghost variables
\overline{z_{k}{:}b_{k}}{\,\dashrightarrow\,}\overline{y_{j}{:}t_{j}}{\,\shortrightarrow\,}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\bigsqcap}}\overline{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{A_{i}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}\,{t_{i}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{A_{i}^{\prime}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}}
,

\displaystyle\Gamma~{\color[rgb]{1,0.2695,0}\vdash\!\![}{A}{\color[rgb]{1,0.2695,0}]}~\overline{z_{k}{:}b_{k}}{\,\dashrightarrow\,}\overline{y_{j}{:}t_{j}}{\,\shortrightarrow\,}\overline{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{A_{i}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}\,{t_{i}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{A_{i}^{\prime}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.2695,0}~\approx\hskip-5.55002pt\text{\Large\T1\guilsinglright}}\overline{z_{k}{:}t_{k}}\implies

\displaystyle\Gamma,\overline{z_{k}{:}t_{k}}\vdash A\subseteq\bigvee A_{i}\land

\displaystyle\Gamma,\overline{z_{k}{:}t_{k}}\vdash\overline{z_{k}{:}b_{k}}{\,\dashrightarrow\,}\overline{y_{j}{:}t_{j}}{\,\shortrightarrow\,}\overline{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{A_{i}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}\,{t_{i}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{A_{i}^{\prime}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}}<:\overline{y_{j}{:}t_{j}}{\,\shortrightarrow\,}\overline{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{A_{i}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}\,{t_{i}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{A_{i}^{\prime}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}}

We need three auxiliary lemmas about declarative typing, which
establish that we can drop unused binding from the type context,
well-typed substitution preserves typing judgement, and
well-formedness implies subtyping.

Lemma E.4.

[Strengthening] Given a type context
\Gamma
, term
e
and HAT
\tau
, for any free variable
x
and pure refinement type
t
:

\Gamma,x{:}t\vdash e:\tau\land\Gamma\vdash^{\textbf{WF}}\tau{\implies}\Gamma\vdash e:\tau

Lemma E.5.

[Substitution Preserves Typing] Given a type context
\Gamma
, term
e
and HAT
\tau
, for any free variable
x
and pure refinement type
t
:

\Gamma,x{:}t\vdash e:\tau\land\Gamma\vdash v:t{\implies}\Gamma\vdash e:\tau[x\mapsto v]

Lemma E.6.

[Well-formedness implies Subtyping] Given a type context
\Gamma
, term
e
and HAT
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{A}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}\,{t}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{B}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}
, we have

\Gamma\vdash^{\textbf{WF}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{A}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}\,{t}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{B}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}{\implies}\Gamma\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{A}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}\,{t}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{B}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}<:{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{A\land A^{\prime}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}\,{t}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{B\land(A^{\prime};{\square}{\langle}{\top}{\rangle})}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}
.

Proof.

Consider the denotation of HAT
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{A}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}\,{t}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{B}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{A\land A^{\prime}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}\,{t}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{B\land(A^{\prime};{\square}{\langle}{\top}{\rangle})}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}
:

\displaystyle\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{A}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}\,{t}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{B}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}}\rrbracket

\displaystyle=\{e~|~\emptyset\vdash_{{\tt s}}e:\lfloor{t}\rfloor\land\forall\alpha\,\alpha^{\prime}\,v.\;\alpha\in\mathcal{L}({A})\land{\alpha}\vDash{e}{\,\xhookrightarrow{\alpha^{\prime}}\,\hskip-5.0pt^{*}\,}{v}{\implies}v\in\llbracket{t}\rrbracket\land\alpha{\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\begin{picture}\roundcap\put(0.1,0.5){\line{1}{0}{0.8}}\put(0.35,0.1){\line{0}{1}{0.8}}\put(0.65,0.1){\line{0}{1}{0.8}}\end{picture}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\begin{picture}\roundcap\put(0.1,0.5){\line{1}{0}{0.8}}\put(0.35,0.1){\line{0}{1}{0.8}}\put(0.65,0.1){\line{0}{1}{0.8}}\end{picture}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\begin{picture}\roundcap\put(0.1,0.5){\line{1}{0}{0.8}}\put(0.35,0.1){\line{0}{1}{0.8}}\put(0.65,0.1){\line{0}{1}{0.8}}\end{picture}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\begin{picture}\roundcap\put(0.1,0.5){\line{1}{0}{0.8}}\put(0.35,0.1){\line{0}{1}{0.8}}\put(0.65,0.1){\line{0}{1}{0.8}}\end{picture}}}}}}\alpha^{\prime}\in\mathcal{L}({B})\}

\displaystyle\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{A\land A^{\prime}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}\,{t}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{B\land(A^{\prime};{\square}{\langle}{\top}{\rangle})}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}}\rrbracket

\displaystyle=\{e~|~\emptyset\vdash_{{\tt s}}e:\lfloor{t}\rfloor\land\forall\alpha\,\alpha^{\prime}\,v.\;\alpha\in\mathcal{L}({A\land A^{\prime}})\land{\alpha}\vDash{e}{\,\xhookrightarrow{\alpha^{\prime}}\,\hskip-5.0pt^{*}\,}{v}{\implies}

\displaystyle\hskip 17.00024pt\hskip 17.00024ptv\in\llbracket{t}\rrbracket\land\alpha{\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\begin{picture}\roundcap\put(0.1,0.5){\line{1}{0}{0.8}}\put(0.35,0.1){\line{0}{1}{0.8}}\put(0.65,0.1){\line{0}{1}{0.8}}\end{picture}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\begin{picture}\roundcap\put(0.1,0.5){\line{1}{0}{0.8}}\put(0.35,0.1){\line{0}{1}{0.8}}\put(0.65,0.1){\line{0}{1}{0.8}}\end{picture}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\begin{picture}\roundcap\put(0.1,0.5){\line{1}{0}{0.8}}\put(0.35,0.1){\line{0}{1}{0.8}}\put(0.65,0.1){\line{0}{1}{0.8}}\end{picture}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\begin{picture}\roundcap\put(0.1,0.5){\line{1}{0}{0.8}}\put(0.35,0.1){\line{0}{1}{0.8}}\put(0.65,0.1){\line{0}{1}{0.8}}\end{picture}}}}}}\alpha^{\prime}\in\mathcal{L}({B\land(A^{\prime};{\square}{\langle}{\top}{\rangle})})\}

Consider a trace
\alpha\in\mathcal{L}({A\land A^{\prime}})
, it also can be recognized by automata
A
, thus for any
{\alpha}\vDash{e}{\,\xhookrightarrow{\alpha^{\prime}}\,\hskip-5.0pt^{*}\,}{v}
, we have
\alpha{\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\begin{picture}\roundcap\put(0.1,0.5){\line{1}{0}{0.8}}\put(0.35,0.1){\line{0}{1}{0.8}}\put(0.65,0.1){\line{0}{1}{0.8}}\end{picture}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\begin{picture}\roundcap\put(0.1,0.5){\line{1}{0}{0.8}}\put(0.35,0.1){\line{0}{1}{0.8}}\put(0.65,0.1){\line{0}{1}{0.8}}\end{picture}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\begin{picture}\roundcap\put(0.1,0.5){\line{1}{0}{0.8}}\put(0.35,0.1){\line{0}{1}{0.8}}\put(0.65,0.1){\line{0}{1}{0.8}}\end{picture}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\begin{picture}\roundcap\put(0.1,0.5){\line{1}{0}{0.8}}\put(0.35,0.1){\line{0}{1}{0.8}}\put(0.65,0.1){\line{0}{1}{0.8}}\end{picture}}}}}}\alpha^{\prime}\in\mathcal{L}({B})
. Moreover, the trace
\alpha{\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\begin{picture}\roundcap\put(0.1,0.5){\line{1}{0}{0.8}}\put(0.35,0.1){\line{0}{1}{0.8}}\put(0.65,0.1){\line{0}{1}{0.8}}\end{picture}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\begin{picture}\roundcap\put(0.1,0.5){\line{1}{0}{0.8}}\put(0.35,0.1){\line{0}{1}{0.8}}\put(0.65,0.1){\line{0}{1}{0.8}}\end{picture}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\begin{picture}\roundcap\put(0.1,0.5){\line{1}{0}{0.8}}\put(0.35,0.1){\line{0}{1}{0.8}}\put(0.65,0.1){\line{0}{1}{0.8}}\end{picture}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\begin{picture}\roundcap\put(0.1,0.5){\line{1}{0}{0.8}}\put(0.35,0.1){\line{0}{1}{0.8}}\put(0.65,0.1){\line{0}{1}{0.8}}\end{picture}}}}}}\alpha^{\prime}
has a prefix
\alpha
, thus is naturally recognized by automata
(A\land A^{\prime});{\square}{\langle}{\top}{\rangle}
. Thus, we know
\alpha{\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\begin{picture}\roundcap\put(0.1,0.5){\line{1}{0}{0.8}}\put(0.35,0.1){\line{0}{1}{0.8}}\put(0.65,0.1){\line{0}{1}{0.8}}\end{picture}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\begin{picture}\roundcap\put(0.1,0.5){\line{1}{0}{0.8}}\put(0.35,0.1){\line{0}{1}{0.8}}\put(0.65,0.1){\line{0}{1}{0.8}}\end{picture}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\begin{picture}\roundcap\put(0.1,0.5){\line{1}{0}{0.8}}\put(0.35,0.1){\line{0}{1}{0.8}}\put(0.65,0.1){\line{0}{1}{0.8}}\end{picture}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\begin{picture}\roundcap\put(0.1,0.5){\line{1}{0}{0.8}}\put(0.35,0.1){\line{0}{1}{0.8}}\put(0.65,0.1){\line{0}{1}{0.8}}\end{picture}}}}}}\alpha^{\prime}\in\mathcal{L}({(A\land A^{\prime});{\square}{\langle}{\top}{\rangle}\land B})
.
Note that the rule
WFHoare
requires that precondition is always the “prefix” of the postcondition automata, thus
\Gamma\vdash^{\textbf{WF}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{A}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}\,{t}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{B}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}
implies
(A;{\square}{\langle}{\top}{\rangle})\subseteq B
, which means that
\mathcal{L}({(A\land A^{\prime});{\square}{\langle}{\top}{\rangle}\land B})=\mathcal{L}({A^{\prime};{\square}{\langle}{\top}{\rangle}\land B})
. Thus the term
e
is in the denotation of type
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{A\land A^{\prime}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}\,{t}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{B\land(A^{\prime};{\square}{\langle}{\top}{\rangle})}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}
when it is in the denotation of type
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{A}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}\,{t}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{B}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}
.
∎

Now we can prove the soundness theorem of our typing algorithm with respect to our declarative type system. As the type synthesis rules are defined mutually recursively, we simultaneously prove both are correct. The following theorem states a stronger inductive invariant that is sufficient to prove the soundness of the typing algorithm.

Theorem E.7.

[Soundness of the type synthesis and type check algorithm] For all type context

\Gamma
, term
e
, pure refinement type
t
and HAT
\tau
,

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash e{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}~\Leftarrow~}\tau\implies\Gamma\vdash e:\tau

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash e{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}~\Leftarrow~}t\implies\Gamma\vdash e:t

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash e{\color[rgb]{1,0.2695,0}~\Rightarrow~}\tau\implies\Gamma\vdash e:\tau

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash e{\color[rgb]{1,0.2695,0}~\Rightarrow~}t\implies\Gamma\vdash e:t

Proof.

We proceed by induction over the mutual recursive structure of type synthesis (
{\color[rgb]{1,0.2695,0}~\Rightarrow~}
) and type check (
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}~\Leftarrow~}
) rules. In the cases for; synthesis and checking rules of rule
SynConst
,
SynBaseVar
,
SynFuncVar
,
ChkGhost
,
ChkInter
,
ChkFunc
,
ChkFix
,
ChkMatch
, the typing rules in
Fig. 14
align exactly with these rules. There are seven remaining cases that are about let-bindings (
ChkLetV
and
ChkLetE
), application (
ChkApp
,
ChkPOpApp
, and
ChkEOpApp
), and subtyping (
ChkSubV
and
ChkSub
). The rule
ChkLetV
,
ChkPOpApp
, and
ChkSubV
are less interesting, which are similar with standard refinement typing algorithm
(
Jhala and Vazou, 2021
)
and simply drop the pre- and post-condition automata from their effectful version. Consequently, we show the proof of three interesting cases (
ChkSub
,
ChkLetE
,
ChkEOpApp
) below, where the case
ChkApp
is almost the same as
ChkEOpApp
.

Case

ChkSub
:

ChkSub

\Gamma\vdash^{\textbf{WF}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{A_{2}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}\,{t_{2}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{B_{2}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}

\Gamma\vdash e{\color[rgb]{1,0.2695,0}~\Rightarrow~}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{A_{1}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}\,{t_{1}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{B_{1}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}

\Gamma\vdash A_{2}<:A_{1}

\Gamma\vdash t_{1}<:t_{2}

\Gamma\vdash(A_{2};{\square}{\langle}{\top}{\rangle})\land B_{1}\subseteq(A_{2};{\square}{\langle}{\top}{\rangle})\land B_{2}

\Gamma\vdash e{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}~\Leftarrow~}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{A_{2}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}\,{t_{2}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{B_{2}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}

This rule connects the relation between the type synthesis and type check judgement. From the induction hypothesis and the precondition of
ChkSub
, we know

(1)

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash A_{2}<:A_{1}\ \

from precondition

(2)

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash t_{1}<:t_{2}\ \

from precondition

(3)

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash(A_{2};{\square}{\langle}{\top}{\rangle})\land B_{1}\subseteq(A_{2};{\square}{\langle}{\top}{\rangle})\land B_{2}\ \

from precondition

(4)

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{A_{1}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}\,{t_{1}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{B_{1}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}<:{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{A_{2}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}\,{t_{2}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{B_{2}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}\ \

via rule
SubHoare
and (1) (2) (3)

(5)

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash e:{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{A_{1}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}\,{t_{1}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{B_{1}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}\ \

inductive hypothesis

(6)

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash e:{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{A_{2}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}\,{t_{2}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{B_{2}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}\ \

via rule
TSub
and (4) (5)

Then the proof immediate holds in this case.

Case

ChkLetE
:

ChkLetE

\Gamma\vdash^{\textbf{WF}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{A}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}\,{t}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{B}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}

\Gamma\vdash e_{x}{\color[rgb]{1,0.2695,0}~\Rightarrow~}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\bigsqcap}}\overline{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{A_{i}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}\,{t_{i}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{A_{i}^{\prime}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}}

Decidability of Algorithmic Typing

We present the proof for
Theorem 5.2
from
Sec. 5
. The proof requires the following lemmas about the
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2773,0.6719}{\tt SFAInclusion}}
subroutine and the instantiation function
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2773,0.6719}{\tt Abduce}}
.

Lemma E.8.

[Decidability of Base Subtyping] the
\Gamma\vdash{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mbox{\rm{\lx@text@lbrace}}}{\nu{:}{b}\,|\,{\phi_{1}}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mbox{\rm{\lx@text@rbrace}}}<:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mbox{\rm{\lx@text@lbrace}}}{\nu{:}{b}\,|\,{\phi_{2}}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mbox{\rm{\lx@text@rbrace}}}
is decidable.

Proof.

As we mentioned in Section 5.1, similar to standard refinement type system, the subtyping check will be encoded as a query in EPR, which is in the decidable fragment of FOL. Thus, the base subtyping check is decidable.
∎

Lemma E.9.

[Decidability of Inclusion Algorithm] the
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2773,0.6719}{\tt SFAInclusion}}
is decidable.

Proof.

The gathered literals are finite, thus the induced boolean combination is also finite. Then the loop in
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2773,0.6719}{\tt SFAInclusion}}
(line
3
in
Algorithm 1
) has a finite bound. According to Lemma
E.8
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2773,0.6719}{\tt SFAInclusion}}
is decidable.
∎

Lemma E.10.

[Decidability of Instantiation Algorithm] the
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2773,0.6719}{\tt Abduce}}
is decidable.

Proof.

The gathered literals are finite, thus the induced boolean combination is also finite. Then the loop in
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2773,0.6719}{\tt Abduce}}
(line
6
in
Algorithm 1
) has a finite bound. According to Lemma
E.8
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2773,0.6719}{\tt Abduce}}
is decidable.
∎

Lemma E.11.

[Decidability of Well-Formedness] For all type context
\Gamma
, type
t
, and
\tau
, the
\Gamma\vdash^{\textbf{WF}}t
and
\Gamma\vdash^{\textbf{WF}}\tau
is decidable.

Proof.

According to the well-formedness rules shown in
Fig. 13
, these rules are inductively defined and structural decreasing over the types (
t_{1}
or
\tau_{1}
). Thus, they always terminate.
∎

Lemma E.12.

[Decidability of Subtyping] For all type context
\Gamma
, type
t_{1}
,
t_{2}
,
\tau_{1}
, and
\tau_{2}
, the
\Gamma\vdash t_{1}<:t_{2}
and
\Gamma\vdash\tau_{1}<:\tau_{2}
is decidable.

Proof.

According to the subtyping rules shown in
Fig. 13
, Lemma
E.8
and Lemma
E.9
, these rules are inductively defined and structural decreasing over the types (
t_{1}
or
\tau_{1}
). Thus, they always terminate.
∎

Lemma E.13.

[Decidability of Instantiation] For all type context
\Gamma
and automata
A
,

\Gamma~{\color[rgb]{1,0.2695,0}\vdash\!\![}{A}{\color[rgb]{1,0.2695,0}]}~\overline{z_{k}{:}b_{k}}{\,\dashrightarrow\,}\overline{y_{j}{:}t_{j}}{\,\shortrightarrow\,}\tau_{x}{\color[rgb]{1,0.2695,0}~\approx\hskip-6.00006pt\text{\Large\T1\guilsinglright}}\overline{z_{k}{:}t_{k}}
is decidable.

Proof.

According to the rule shown in
Fig. 13
and Lemma
E.10
, it always terminates.
∎

Now we can prove the decidability theorem of our typing algorithm. As the type synthesis rules are defined mutually recursively, we simultaneously prove both are decidable. The following theorem states a stronger inductive invariant that is sufficient to prove the decidability of the typing algorithm.

Theorem E.14 (Decidability of
Algorithmic Typing).

Type checking a term
e
against a type

\tau
in a typing context
\Gamma
, all
\Gamma\vdash e{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}~\Leftarrow~}\tau
,
\Gamma\vdash v{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}~\Leftarrow~}t
,
\Gamma\vdash e{\color[rgb]{1,0.2695,0}~\Rightarrow~}\tau
, and
\Gamma\vdash v{\color[rgb]{1,0.2695,0}~\Rightarrow~}t
is decidable.

Proof.

According to Lemma
E.11
, Lemma
E.12
, and Lemma
E.13
, the auxiliary functions (well-formedness, subtyping, instantiation) always terminate.
Thus, the key of the decidability proof is finding a ranking function over the inputs of typing algorithm, which is based on the size of terms (
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2773,0.6719}{\tt TermSize}}
) and types (
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2773,0.6719}{\tt TypeSize}}
):

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.2773,0.6719}{\tt TermSize}}(c)\

\displaystyle\doteq 1

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.2773,0.6719}{\tt TermSize}}(x)\

\displaystyle\doteq 1

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.2773,0.6719}{\tt TermSize}}(d(\overline{v_{i}}))\

\displaystyle\doteq 1+\Sigma_{i}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2773,0.6719}{\tt TermSize}}(v_{i})

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.2773,0.6719}{\tt TermSize}}(\lambda{x}{:}{t}.{e})\

\displaystyle\doteq 1+{\color[rgb]{0,0.2773,0.6719}{\tt TermSize}}(e)

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.2773,0.6719}{\tt TermSize}}({\color[rgb]{0.0313,0.0039,0.457}\boldsymbol{\mathtt{fix}}}{f}{:}{t}.\lambda{x}{:}{t}.{e})\

\displaystyle\doteq 2+{\color[rgb]{0,0.2773,0.6719}{\tt TermSize}}(e)

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.2773,0.6719}{\tt TermSize}}({\color[rgb]{0.0313,0.0039,0.457}\boldsymbol{\mathtt{let}}}\ {x}\ \mathtt{=}\ {\mathbf{op}\ \overline{v_{i}}}\ {\color[rgb]{0.0313,0.0039,0.457}\boldsymbol{\mathtt{in}}}\ {e})\

\displaystyle\doteq 1+{\color[rgb]{0,0.2773,0.6719}{\tt TermSize}}(e)+\Sigma_{i}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2773,0.6719}{\tt TermSize}}(v_{i})

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.2773,0.6719}{\tt TermSize}}({\color[rgb]{0.0313,0.0039,0.457}\boldsymbol{\mathtt{let}}}\ {x}\ \mathtt{=}\ {op\ \overline{v_{1}}}\ {\color[rgb]{0.0313,0.0039,0.457}\boldsymbol{\mathtt{in}}}\ {e})\

\displaystyle\doteq 1+{\color[rgb]{0,0.2773,0.6719}{\tt TermSize}}(e)+\Sigma_{i}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2773,0.6719}{\tt TermSize}}(v_{i})

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.2773,0.6719}{\tt TermSize}}({\color[rgb]{0.0313,0.0039,0.457}\boldsymbol{\mathtt{let}}}\ {x}\ \mathtt{=}\ {v_{1}\ v_{2}}\ {\color[rgb]{0.0313,0.0039,0.457}\boldsymbol{\mathtt{in}}}\ {e})\

\displaystyle\doteq 1+{\color[rgb]{0,0.2773,0.6719}{\tt TermSize}}(e)+{\color[rgb]{0,0.2773,0.6719}{\tt TermSize}}(v_{1})+{\color[rgb]{0,0.2773,0.6719}{\tt TermSize}}(v_{2})

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.2773,0.6719}{\tt TermSize}}({\color[rgb]{0.0313,0.0039,0.457}\boldsymbol{\mathtt{let}}}\ {x}\ \mathtt{=}\ {e_{1}}\ {\color[rgb]{0.0313,0.0039,0.457}\boldsymbol{\mathtt{in}}}\ {e_{2}})\

\displaystyle\doteq 1+{\color[rgb]{0,0.2773,0.6719}{\tt TermSize}}(e_{1})+{\color[rgb]{0,0.2773,0.6719}{\tt TermSize}}(e_{2})

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.2773,0.6719}{\tt TermSize}}({\color[rgb]{0.0313,0.0039,0.457}\boldsymbol{\mathtt{match}}}\ {v}\ {\color[rgb]{0.0313,0.0039,0.457}\boldsymbol{\mathtt{with}}}\ {\overline{d\ \overline{y}{\,\shortrightarrow\,}{e_{i}}}})\

\displaystyle\doteq 1+{\color[rgb]{0,0.2773,0.6719}{\tt TermSize}}v+\Sigma_{i}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2773,0.6719}{\tt TermSize}}(e_{i})

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.2773,0.6719}{\tt TypeSize}}({\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mbox{\rm{\lx@text@lbrace}}}{\nu{:}{b}\,|\,{\phi}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mbox{\rm{\lx@text@rbrace}}})\

\displaystyle\doteq 1

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.2773,0.6719}{\tt TypeSize}}(x{:}t{\,\shortrightarrow\,}\tau)\

\displaystyle\doteq 1+{\color[rgb]{0,0.2773,0.6719}{\tt TypeSize}}(t)+{\color[rgb]{0,0.2773,0.6719}{\tt TypeSize}}(\tau)

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.2773,0.6719}{\tt TypeSize}}(x{:}b{\,\dashrightarrow\,}\tau)\

\displaystyle\doteq 1+{\color[rgb]{0,0.2773,0.6719}{\tt TypeSize}}(\tau)

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.2773,0.6719}{\tt TypeSize}}({\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{A}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}\,{t}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{A}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{})\

\displaystyle\doteq 1+{\color[rgb]{0,0.2773,0.6719}{\tt TypeSize}}(t)

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.2773,0.6719}{\tt TypeSize}}(\tau_{1}\,\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\sqcap}}\,\tau_{2})\

\displaystyle\doteq 1+{\color[rgb]{0,0.2773,0.6719}{\tt TypeSize}}(\tau_{1})+{\color[rgb]{0,0.2773,0.6719}{\tt TypeSize}}(\tau_{2})

With the help of
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2773,0.6719}{\tt TermSize}}
, we define a lexicographic ranking function as a string contains two natural numbers (a pair of natural numbers).

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.2773,0.6719}{\tt Rank}}(\Gamma\vdash e{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}~\Leftarrow~}\tau)\

\displaystyle\doteq({\color[rgb]{0,0.2773,0.6719}{\tt TermSize}}(e),{\color[rgb]{0,0.2773,0.6719}{\tt TypeSize}}(\tau))

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.2773,0.6719}{\tt Rank}}(\Gamma\vdash e{\color[rgb]{1,0.2695,0}~\Rightarrow~}\tau)\

\displaystyle\doteq({\color[rgb]{0,0.2773,0.6719}{\tt TermSize}}(e),0)

where one rank is less than another iff

\displaystyle(a_{1},b_{1})<(a_{2},b_{2})\iff a_{1}<b_{1}\lor(a_{1}=b_{1}\land b_{1}<b_{2})

We proceed by induction over the mutual recursive structure of type synthesis (
{\color[rgb]{1,0.2695,0}~\Rightarrow~}
) and type check (
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}~\Leftarrow~}
) rules. In the cases for; synthesis rule
SynConst
,
SynBaseVar
, and
SynFuncVar
don’t have recursive call, thus always terminates. Consequently, we try to prove that the rank current call is greater than recursive calls in the rest of cases below.

\Gamma~{\color[rgb]{1,0.2695,0}\vdash\!\![}{A}{\color[rgb]{1,0.2695,0}]}~\overline{z_{k}{:}b_{k}}{\,\dashrightarrow\,}y{:}t_{y}{\,\shortrightarrow\,}\tau_{x}{\color[rgb]{1,0.2695,0}~\approx\hskip-5.10011pt\text{\Large\T1\guilsinglright}}\overline{z_{k}{:}t_{k}}

\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\bigsqcap}}\overline{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{A_{i}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}\,{t_{i}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{A_{i}^{\prime}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}}=\tau_{x}[y\mapsto v_{y}]

\forall i.\Gamma,\overline{z_{k}{:}t_{k}},x{:}t_{i}\vdash e{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}~\Leftarrow~}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{(A;{\square}{\langle}{\top}{\rangle})\land A_{i}^{\prime}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}\,{t}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{B}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}

\Gamma\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.0313,0.0039,0.457}\boldsymbol{\mathtt{let}}}\ {x}\ \mathtt{=}\ {v_{1}\ v_{2}}\ {\color[rgb]{0.0313,0.0039,0.457}\boldsymbol{\mathtt{in}}}\ {e}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}~\Leftarrow~}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{A}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}\,{t}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{B}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}

We the rank current call is

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.2773,0.6719}{\tt Rank}}(\Gamma\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.0313,0.0039,0.457}\boldsymbol{\mathtt{let}}}\ {x}\ \mathtt{=}\ {v_{1}\ v_{2}}\ {\color[rgb]{0.0313,0.0039,0.457}\boldsymbol{\mathtt{in}}}\ {e}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}~\Leftarrow~}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{A}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}\,{t}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{B}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{})

\displaystyle=(1+{\color[rgb]{0,0.2773,0.6719}{\tt TermSize}}(e)+{\color[rgb]{0,0.2773,0.6719}{\tt TermSize}}(v_{1})+{\color[rgb]{0,0.2773,0.6719}{\tt TermSize}}(v_{2}),1+{\color[rgb]{0,0.2773,0.6719}{\tt TypeSize}}(t))

which is greater than recursive calls:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.2773,0.6719}{\tt Rank}}(\Gamma\vdash v_{1}{\color[rgb]{1,0.2695,0}~\Rightarrow~}\overline{z_{k}{:}b_{k}}{\,\dashrightarrow\,}y{:}t_{y}{\,\shortrightarrow\,}\tau_{x})

\displaystyle=({\color[rgb]{0,0.2773,0.6719}{\tt TermSize}}(v_{1}),0)

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.2773,0.6719}{\tt Rank}}(\Gamma\vdash v_{2}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}~\Leftarrow~}t_{y})

\displaystyle=({\color[rgb]{0,0.2773,0.6719}{\tt TermSize}}(v_{2}),{\color[rgb]{0,0.2773,0.6719}{\tt TypeSize}}(t_{y}))

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.2773,0.6719}{\tt Rank}}(\Gamma,\overline{z_{k}{:}t_{k}},x{:}t_{i}\vdash e{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}~\Leftarrow~}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{(A;{\square}{\langle}{\top}{\rangle})\land A_{i}^{\prime}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}\,{t}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{B}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{})

\displaystyle=({\color[rgb]{0,0.2773,0.6719}{\tt TermSize}}(e),1+{\color[rgb]{0,0.2773,0.6719}{\tt TypeSize}}(t))

is fresh

Case

ChkSubV
:

ChkSubV

\Gamma\vdash^{\textbf{WF}}t

\Gamma\vdash v{\color[rgb]{1,0.2695,0}~\Rightarrow~}t^{\prime}

\Gamma\vdash t^{\prime}<:t

\Gamma\vdash v{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}~\Leftarrow~}t

We the rank current call is

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.2773,0.6719}{\tt Rank}}(\Gamma\vdash v{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}~\Leftarrow~}t)

\displaystyle=({\color[rgb]{0,0.2773,0.6719}{\tt TermSize}}(v),{\color[rgb]{0,0.2773,0.6719}{\tt TypeSize}}(t))

which is greater than recursive calls:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.2773,0.6719}{\tt Rank}}(\Gamma\vdash v{\color[rgb]{1,0.2695,0}~\Rightarrow~}t^{\prime})

\displaystyle=({\color[rgb]{0,0.2773,0.6719}{\tt TermSize}}(v),0)

Thus, the typing algorithm is decidable.
